Or, like I mentioned, when I'm playing bridge with the ladies. I'll be shuffling the deck—we use those nice plastic-coated cards now because Mary always manages to spill her iced tea, bless her heart—and I just keep feeling like every single woman at that table has her eyes completely glued to my arm. \n\nIt’s like it has become the very first thing they notice about me now. Before, they'd notice my outfit, or my hair, or they'd ask about my daughter's new house, but now... I just feel like they see this awful scar before they even see me as a person. So, even though I know I might be exaggerating how terrible it looks in my own head, the feeling that everyone else is noticing it first and foremost is just... well, it's completely overwhelming. It just takes over everything.\n\nDoctor: When you're most upset about the scar, is it more because you feel others are judging your appearance, or because you yourself feel disgusted or upset by how it looks?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, Doctor, I feel like we might be going around in circles just a little bit, aren't we? I'm trying my best to explain it, I really am, it's just... well, let me try to put it another way, because I want to make sure I'm giving you what you need. \n\nIf I really have to choose between the two things you just mentioned... I suppose the part that truly upsets me the most, the part that just ties my stomach up in absolute knots, is the other people. Because, like I've been saying, I do have enough sense to know that the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it looks to me. I really do know that. My logical brain—the one that's spent forty years making sure the debits and credits match up perfectly at the end of the month—knows I'm probably being a bit hard on myself when I'm alone in my bathroom looking at it. \n\nBut knowing that doesn't stop the feeling I get when I'm actually out in the world. When I’m at church on Sunday, or when I'm sitting there at bridge club with the girls... I just keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right straight to it. It doesn't matter if I logically know it's just a mark, because in that moment, I feel like it’s the very first thing they notice about me now. It's like it completely erases the rest of me. Before they see my face, before they ask how my week was, they just see this flaw. So, yes, I am upset by how it looks, but it's that terrible, overwhelming feeling of being stared at—of it being the only thing people focus on when I'm just trying to go about my normal life—that really brings me to tears.\n\nDoctor: So, when you're most upset about the scar, would you say it's mainly because you feel others are judging you for it, or because you yourself can't stand how it looks no matter where you are or who's around?\n\nPatient: Oh, dear me... Doctor, I really do feel terrible, I must be doing a dreadful job of explaining myself today, because we just keep circling right back to the exact same thing, don't we? I'm so sorry, I really am trying to give you the specific answer you're looking for, but I just don't think I can separate it out quite as neatly as you want me to. \n\nIf you're asking if I can't stand it *no matter where I am* or who's around... well, that's not quite right either. Because when I'm just sitting at home in my recliner in the evenings, say, watching Jeopardy with a cup of chamomile tea, or working on the Sunday crossword puzzle—I always do the crossword in pen, my late husband used to tease me endlessly about that, but working with numbers all day just makes you confident in your penmanship, I suppose—anyway, when I'm alone like that, I don't like the scar, certainly, but I can tolerate it. I can look down at my wrist and think, 'Well, there's that ugly mark,' and then I just go right back to my puzzle. \n\nSo it's not that I'm just sitting there in a vacuum, completely unable to stand the sight of myself when I'm all alone. It really is the being around others that makes it so unbearable. But like I've been trying to say, it’s not that I think they’re judging my character or thinking I'm a wicked person. It’s just that awful, heavy feeling that their eyes are locked onto my wrist instead of my face. I just don't know how else to describe it to you, I really don't. I don't know if that's them judging me or me just being disgusted, but it's the being looked at that makes me feel so terribly exposed and upset. I just wish I could give you a simpler answer!\n\nDoctor: When you avoid activities like church or bridge club, is it mainly to prevent people from seeing the scar, or do you also worry about how they might judge you as a person if they do see it?\n\nPatient: Well, Doctor... again, I really don't think they're judging me as a person. I don't sit there and think, 'Oh, the ladies are going to think I'm a terrible, wicked woman because of this.' It's not my character I'm worried about. It really, truly is just about preventing them from seeing it in the first place. \n\nAnd it's getting to be such a problem, it really is... to give you an idea of how much this is getting in the way, it's not even just church and bridge club anymore. I've actually been late turning in some of my bookkeeping work lately. And I *never* do that. I have three small local businesses I do the ledgers for, and I pride myself on always, always having their reconciliations done by the third of the month, like clockwork. But lately... well, I've also skipped out on my choir commitments the last couple of weeks. I sing alto, and we are right in the middle of preparing for the spring cantata, which is just going to be gorgeous this year, but I just couldn't bring myself to go. Same with the community theater down on Main Street—I usually volunteer to run the box office or do the ushering on Friday nights, and I've skipped those commitments entirely, too. \n\nAnd it's all because... well, I just get so incredibly caught up at home before I even leave the house. I'll be standing in front of my closet for what feels like hours, trying on different cardigans, trying to find a blouse with cuffs that are tight enough, just doing everything I can to make absolutely sure that my wrist won’t be noticed if I reach out to hand someone a theater program or if I have to hold my sheet music up in the choir loft. I spend so much time fussing and worrying and trying to hide it that I just run out of time and energy, and I end up calling in with some silly excuse and staying home. So, you see, it really is just the fear of the scar being seen, plain and simple.\n\nDoctor: When you imagine people seeing your scar, do you worry they might think less of you as a person, or is it more that you feel the scar is so noticeable that it makes you feel embarrassed or self-conscious, regardless of what they actually think?\n\nPatient: Oh, Doctor, bless your heart, but I really do feel like a broken record today! I know you probably have to ask these things in a certain way for your medical files—it’s just like when I have to ask my bookkeeping clients for the third time to please, please separate their personal receipts from their business ones, they get a little frustrated with me, too—but yes, it is absolutely the second thing you just said. \n\nI don't sit there worrying that they think less of my character. It’s entirely about the embarrassment. Like I’ve been trying to say, I do know the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it looks to me. I know that in my logical mind. But it just feels so glaringly noticeable to me that I can't shake the self-consciousness, regardless of what's actually going on in their heads. When I’m at church on Sunday mornings, or sitting around the card table at bridge, I just keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it. \n\nIt’s this overwhelming sensation that it’s the very first thing they notice about me now. Instead of seeing me, or asking about my grandchildren, it just feels like this big, flashing neon sign on my arm. Just the thought of someone like Mrs. Higgins at church—she sits right next to me in the alto section and has eyes like a hawk, I swear she notices if you have a single thread out of place on your collar—just the thought of her eyes darting down to my wrist makes my face burn. So, yes, it’s just that awful, self-conscious feeling that it's so incredibly noticeable, not that they think I'm a bad person.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Well, goodness me, Doctor, I feel like I’m talking to a brick wall today! Or maybe I’m just rambling so much that you’re having trouble keeping track of it all, which I suppose I can’t blame you for. My late husband used to say I could talk the ears off a brass monkey, bless his heart. But yes, like I was saying just a few minutes ago... it absolutely has caused me to avoid my responsibilities. 

I mentioned the bookkeeping work earlier—I've actually been late turning in the ledgers for those three local businesses I do the accounting for. And I am *never* late. It's just so completely unlike me to miss a deadline like that. And then there are my choir commitments, skipping out on the spring cantata preparations, and the community theater down on Main Street... I've missed all of those volunteer shifts lately, too. 

And it really is all just because I get so terribly caught up at home before I even step out the door. I'll be standing there in my bedroom, trying to make absolutely certain that my wrist won't be noticed. I'll try on three different long-sleeved blouses, and then I'll worry that the cuffs are going to ride up if I have to reach out to hand someone a theater program or turn a page of sheet music, and I just start fussing and worrying. By the time I'm done obsessing over it, I'm already late, I'm exhausted, and I just give up and stay home. So, yes, to answer your question directly, it is definitely getting in the way of my work and my commitments, just like I was saying.

========== INPUT [doctor] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.22, F40.10\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, it’s... I suppose it feels a little foolish to even be here, taking up your time with this, but I just haven't been feeling quite right lately. You see, it’s about this... this scar on my wrist. I know, logically, it’s probably not as bad as it looks to me. I’m a rational person, I've done bookkeeping and accounting work for years, so I deal with facts and figures all day, and I *know* it’s just a mark. But... well, it’s just gotten to the point where it's constantly on my mind. \n\nWhen I’m out, especially—like when I’m at church on Sundays, or on Tuesday afternoons when I get together with my bridge group. I just keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it. I’ll be sitting there holding my cards, or reaching out to shake someone's hand during the service, and it just feels like it’s the very first thing they notice about me now. I try to pull my sleeves down, or I try to keep my hand tucked under the table, but then I start worrying that I'm just drawing more attention to myself by fidgeting with my clothes. It’s just so exhausting, feeling like people are staring at it, even though a part of me keeps trying to tell myself they probably aren't... but I just can't shake the feeling that it's all anyone sees when they look at me.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about your scar affected your ability to participate in activities you usually enjoy, or caused you significant distress during the day?\n\nPatient: Well... yes, I suppose it has, and that’s really what bothers me the most about all this. I’ve always prided myself on being very reliable, you know? I do bookkeeping for a few small businesses in town—just keeping the ledgers balanced, doing the payroll, that sort of thing. I've done it for years. But lately, I’ll sit down at my desk to work, and I just... I get so distracted. I’ll spend an hour just trying on different long-sleeved blouses, or pulling out my old jewelry boxes to test out various watches and thick bracelets, just trying to see what covers the mark best when I move my hand. And because I get so caught up trying to make absolutely sure my wrist won’t be noticed, I’ve actually been late turning in my bookkeeping work. The owner at the hardware store even called last week to ask if I was feeling alright because I'm never, ever late with his reports. \n\nAnd it’s not just my work, either. It’s the things I actually look forward to. I volunteer with the local community theater—usually I’m taking tickets or handing out the playbills at the front doors—and I sing alto in the church choir. I’ve been with the choir for over a decade, we're like a family. But recently, I’ve just... I’ve been skipping my commitments with them. I call in and make up excuses, say I have a scratchy throat or a bad headache. Because when I think about standing there in the lobby, reaching out to hand a program to a stranger, or holding up my heavy music folder under those bright sanctuary lights... my wrist would be right there, completely exposed for everyone to see. I just get so overwhelmed and anxious trying to figure out how to hide it that it’s easier just to stay home. It breaks my heart to miss the shows and the singing, it really does, but I just can't bear the thought of being up there and feeling like a spectacle.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or self-conscious in social situations generally, even when the scar isn't a factor—like speaking up in a group or eating in front of others?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. No, not at all. You see, I've actually always been quite a social butterfly, really. If you were to ask anyone in my bridge group—especially Martha, she’s known me since our girls were in Girl Scouts together back in the late eighties—she’d tell you I’ve never been shy about speaking my mind or being around folks. \n\nWhether it's discussing the new town budget at the city council meetings, which I always try to attend because numbers are my bread and butter, you know, or just chatting with the ladies at the salon, I’ve always felt perfectly comfortable in a crowd. And as for eating in front of others, oh, that’s never been a problem in the slightest. We have these wonderful potluck dinners at the church every first Sunday of the month. I always bring my green bean casserole—well, I say it's mine, but it's really just my mother's old recipe with a little extra crispy onion on top, but everyone loves it—and I can sit there at those long folding tables in the fellowship hall, eating and laughing with the pastor and the choir director without a second thought. I've never felt awkward about eating or talking or just being myself. \n\nIt’s really just... well, it’s just this silly wrist. If I could just wear a long sleeve that stayed put, or if this mark wasn't there, I'd be my usual outgoing self. I don't get nervous about people looking at *me*, exactly, or judging what I have to say... it's just about them looking at *it*. Does that make sense? Because before this whole thing started bothering me so much, I was always the very first one to raise my hand to organize the holiday bake sale or stand up in front of everyone to give the treasurer's report...\n\nDoctor: When you worry about people noticing your scar, do you feel like they might judge you negatively as a person—like they might think you’re less capable, unattractive, or strange—or do you mostly feel like they’re just noticing the scar without it affecting how they see you overall?\n\nPatient: Well, goodness, doctor, I’m really not sure how to answer that. I don't know if I've ever really stopped to parse out exactly what's going through their heads, to be perfectly honest with you. \n\nI mean, I certainly hope they don't think I'm incapable! The people I work for, like the hardware store owner I mentioned, and I also do the ledgers for the little floral shop down on Main Street—I’ve been doing their books since 2012. Or maybe it was late 2011? No, it was definitely 2012, because that was the year my daughter got married and we used them for the centerpieces, those lovely hydrangeas. Anyway, my point is, the people in this town know my work is impeccable. They know I'm sharp as a tack. So I don't think they look at my wrist and suddenly think I've forgotten how to balance a spreadsheet or run a payroll. \n\nBut as for whether they think I'm unattractive or strange... I just don't know. That’s the thing that really gets my mind spinning, you see. I just don't know *what* they're thinking. I'll be at the teller window at the bank, handing over a stack of deposit slips for one of my clients, and I’ll see the teller's eyes flick down to my hand. And I just freeze. Are they disgusted? Are they wondering what on earth happened to me? Do they think I'm unkempt or careless to have a mark like that right out in the open? \n\nI couldn't tell you for sure if they're judging my whole character, or if they're just surprised by the scar and then moving on. I really, truly don't know. I just know I can't stand the thought of their eyes lingering on it. It makes me feel so exposed. It’s like... well, it’s like all the good things about me, all my years of hard work and my nice outfits and the fact that I'm a good neighbor, it all just fades into the background, and all that's left for them to see is this... this flaw. So I suppose I don't really have a good answer for you about whether they're judging me as a person. I just know that the looking itself is what I can't abide.\n\nDoctor: When you think about how much the scar bothers you, does it feel like it's about the way the scar looks, or is it more about what you imagine people might think or do when they see it?\n\nPatient: Oh, my... well, that’s a very nuanced question, isn't it? I’m really not sure how to separate the two, if I'm being completely honest. I don't think I've ever really thought to untangle it like that. \n\nI mean, on the one hand, I look at it myself and I just... I despise how it looks. I’ll be standing in my master bathroom—we actually just remodeled it a few years ago, put in those very bright, daylight LED bulbs around the vanity because the old fixtures were from the nineties and were so terribly dim I couldn't see a thing when I was putting on my moisturizer in the mornings—and I’ll catch sight of my wrist in that harsh, unforgiving light. And it just looks so glaring and awful to me. Just the physical appearance of it bothers me to my very core. I'll just stand there by the sink, sometimes for ten or fifteen minutes, rubbing my thumb over it and thinking about how it just ruins the whole line of my arm. \n\nBut then, on the exact same day, I’ll be down at the grocery store... say I'm at the deli counter waiting on a half pound of sliced turkey, the mesquite smoked kind because that's the only one that makes a decent sandwich for lunch, in my opinion... and I'll reach up to take the little plastic bag over the counter, and I'll realize my sleeve has ridden up. And suddenly my heart is just in my throat, pounding away, because I'm absolutely paralyzed by the thought of the deli clerk or the woman with the shopping cart behind me catching a glimpse of it. \n\nSo is it the look of it that upsets me, or the fear of what they'll think when they see it? I really, truly couldn't tell you which one is driving the bus, so to speak. They just bleed right into one another. I don't know if it's my own eyes I'm worried about or everyone else's, because it just all feels like one big, heavy cloud hanging over me all the time, no matter where I am or who happens to be looking. I'm sorry I can't give you a more definitive answer, it's just... well, it's all just so jumbled together in my head.\n\nDoctor: When you feel upset about the scar, is your main concern that it makes you look physically unattractive, or that people will judge you negatively or think poorly of you because of it?\n\nPatient: Well... gosh, it’s funny you ask it quite like that, because... well, logically, in my rational mind—the same mind that balances those ledgers down at the floral shop to the very penny—I do know that the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it looks to me. I mean, I have enough common sense to realize I might be blowing the physical appearance of it a tiny bit out of proportion when I'm standing there in my bathroom staring at it under those bright LED lights. \n\nBut... oh, it’s just so hard to shake the feeling when I'm actually out and about. When I’m sitting there at church, you know, maybe reaching out to pass the collection plate, or when I’m hosting bridge club on a Tuesday afternoon—I always put out these little cucumber sandwiches with the crusts cut off, just the way Martha likes them, and you have to reach across the card table to deal the hands—I just keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right straight to it. It’s like it has become the very first thing they notice about me now. \n\nSo I suppose it's not exactly that I think they're judging my character, or thinking I'm a bad person or a fraud because of it. It’s just... it makes me feel physically flawed in a way that overtakes everything else. They used to look at me and see a good bookkeeper, a good friend, someone who keeps a tidy home and wears nice blouses. Now, I just feel like I'm reduced to this one ugly mark, and that's all they're looking at. Even if I know, deep down, it's probably not as glaring to the rest of the world as it is to me, I just sit there at the bridge table feeling like their eyes are completely magnetized to my wrist, and it just makes me feel so terribly unattractive and exposed.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your main concern is that the scar looks unattractive to you, or that others will notice it and judge you negatively?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I suppose it really just comes back to exactly what I was trying to explain a moment ago. If I really have to pin it down for you... well, I do know, logically speaking—with that practical bookkeeping brain of mine that I've relied on all these years—that the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it looks to me when I'm standing there analyzing it. I really do have the common sense to know that. \n\nBut the real crux of the issue, the thing that just eats away at me, is that when I'm out doing my normal activities, I just can't shake the feeling that everyone is looking at it. Like when I'm at church on Sunday mornings... we always sit in the third pew on the left, right near the big stained glass window that gets all that bright morning sun, and the minute I reach out to grab the hymnal from the back of the pew, I just feel this absolute certainty that everyone’s eyes go right straight to my wrist. Or, like I mentioned, when I'm playing bridge with the ladies. I'll be shuffling the deck—we use those nice plastic-coated cards now because Mary always manages to spill her iced tea, bless her heart—and I just keep feeling like every single woman at that table has her eyes completely glued to my arm. \n\nIt’s like it has become the very first thing they notice about me now. Before, they'd notice my outfit, or my hair, or they'd ask about my daughter's new house, but now... I just feel like they see this awful scar before they even see me as a person. So, even though I know I might be exaggerating how terrible it looks in my own head, the feeling that everyone else is noticing it first and foremost is just... well, it's completely overwhelming. It just takes over everything.\n\nDoctor: When you're most upset about the scar, is it more because you feel others are judging your appearance, or because you yourself feel disgusted or upset by how it looks?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, Doctor, I feel like we might be going around in circles just a little bit, aren't we? I'm trying my best to explain it, I really am, it's just... well, let me try to put it another way, because I want to make sure I'm giving you what you need. \n\nIf I really have to choose between the two things you just mentioned... I suppose the part that truly upsets me the most, the part that just ties my stomach up in absolute knots, is the other people. Because, like I've been saying, I do have enough sense to know that the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it looks to me. I really do know that. My logical brain—the one that's spent forty years making sure the debits and credits match up perfectly at the end of the month—knows I'm probably being a bit hard on myself when I'm alone in my bathroom looking at it. \n\nBut knowing that doesn't stop the feeling I get when I'm actually out in the world. When I’m at church on Sunday, or when I'm sitting there at bridge club with the girls... I just keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right straight to it. It doesn't matter if I logically know it's just a mark, because in that moment, I feel like it’s the very first thing they notice about me now. It's like it completely erases the rest of me. Before they see my face, before they ask how my week was, they just see this flaw. So, yes, I am upset by how it looks, but it's that terrible, overwhelming feeling of being stared at—of it being the only thing people focus on when I'm just trying to go about my normal life—that really brings me to tears.\n\nDoctor: So, when you're most upset about the scar, would you say it's mainly because you feel others are judging you for it, or because you yourself can't stand how it looks no matter where you are or who's around?\n\nPatient: Oh, dear me... Doctor, I really do feel terrible, I must be doing a dreadful job of explaining myself today, because we just keep circling right back to the exact same thing, don't we? I'm so sorry, I really am trying to give you the specific answer you're looking for, but I just don't think I can separate it out quite as neatly as you want me to. \n\nIf you're asking if I can't stand it *no matter where I am* or who's around... well, that's not quite right either. Because when I'm just sitting at home in my recliner in the evenings, say, watching Jeopardy with a cup of chamomile tea, or working on the Sunday crossword puzzle—I always do the crossword in pen, my late husband used to tease me endlessly about that, but working with numbers all day just makes you confident in your penmanship, I suppose—anyway, when I'm alone like that, I don't like the scar, certainly, but I can tolerate it. I can look down at my wrist and think, 'Well, there's that ugly mark,' and then I just go right back to my puzzle. \n\nSo it's not that I'm just sitting there in a vacuum, completely unable to stand the sight of myself when I'm all alone. It really is the being around others that makes it so unbearable. But like I've been trying to say, it’s not that I think they’re judging my character or thinking I'm a wicked person. It’s just that awful, heavy feeling that their eyes are locked onto my wrist instead of my face. I just don't know how else to describe it to you, I really don't. I don't know if that's them judging me or me just being disgusted, but it's the being looked at that makes me feel so terribly exposed and upset. I just wish I could give you a simpler answer!\n\nDoctor: When you avoid activities like church or bridge club, is it mainly to prevent people from seeing the scar, or do you also worry about how they might judge you as a person if they do see it?\n\nPatient: Well, Doctor... again, I really don't think they're judging me as a person. I don't sit there and think, 'Oh, the ladies are going to think I'm a terrible, wicked woman because of this.' It's not my character I'm worried about. It really, truly is just about preventing them from seeing it in the first place. \n\nAnd it's getting to be such a problem, it really is... to give you an idea of how much this is getting in the way, it's not even just church and bridge club anymore. I've actually been late turning in some of my bookkeeping work lately. And I *never* do that. I have three small local businesses I do the ledgers for, and I pride myself on always, always having their reconciliations done by the third of the month, like clockwork. But lately... well, I've also skipped out on my choir commitments the last couple of weeks. I sing alto, and we are right in the middle of preparing for the spring cantata, which is just going to be gorgeous this year, but I just couldn't bring myself to go. Same with the community theater down on Main Street—I usually volunteer to run the box office or do the ushering on Friday nights, and I've skipped those commitments entirely, too. \n\nAnd it's all because... well, I just get so incredibly caught up at home before I even leave the house. I'll be standing in front of my closet for what feels like hours, trying on different cardigans, trying to find a blouse with cuffs that are tight enough, just doing everything I can to make absolutely sure that my wrist won’t be noticed if I reach out to hand someone a theater program or if I have to hold my sheet music up in the choir loft. I spend so much time fussing and worrying and trying to hide it that I just run out of time and energy, and I end up calling in with some silly excuse and staying home. So, you see, it really is just the fear of the scar being seen, plain and simple.\n\nDoctor: When you imagine people seeing your scar, do you worry they might think less of you as a person, or is it more that you feel the scar is so noticeable that it makes you feel embarrassed or self-conscious, regardless of what they actually think?\n\nPatient: Oh, Doctor, bless your heart, but I really do feel like a broken record today! I know you probably have to ask these things in a certain way for your medical files—it’s just like when I have to ask my bookkeeping clients for the third time to please, please separate their personal receipts from their business ones, they get a little frustrated with me, too—but yes, it is absolutely the second thing you just said. \n\nI don't sit there worrying that they think less of my character. It’s entirely about the embarrassment. Like I’ve been trying to say, I do know the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it looks to me. I know that in my logical mind. But it just feels so glaringly noticeable to me that I can't shake the self-consciousness, regardless of what's actually going on in their heads. When I’m at church on Sunday mornings, or sitting around the card table at bridge, I just keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it. \n\nIt’s this overwhelming sensation that it’s the very first thing they notice about me now. Instead of seeing me, or asking about my grandchildren, it just feels like this big, flashing neon sign on my arm. Just the thought of someone like Mrs. Higgins at church—she sits right next to me in the alto section and has eyes like a hawk, I swear she notices if you have a single thread out of place on your collar—just the thought of her eyes darting down to my wrist makes my face burn. So, yes, it’s just that awful, self-conscious feeling that it's so incredibly noticeable, not that they think I'm a bad person.\n\nDoctor: Has worrying about your scar ever caused you to avoid important responsibilities, like work, or to withdraw from relationships with friends or family members?\n\nPatient: Well, goodness me, Doctor, I feel like I’m talking to a brick wall today! Or maybe I’m just rambling so much that you’re having trouble keeping track of it all, which I suppose I can’t blame you for. My late husband used to say I could talk the ears off a brass monkey, bless his heart. But yes, like I was saying just a few minutes ago... it absolutely has caused me to avoid my responsibilities. \n\nI mentioned the bookkeeping work earlier—I've actually been late turning in the ledgers for those three local businesses I do the accounting for. And I am *never* late. It's just so completely unlike me to miss a deadline like that. And then there are my choir commitments, skipping out on the spring cantata preparations, and the community theater down on Main Street... I've missed all of those volunteer shifts lately, too. \n\nAnd it really is all just because I get so terribly caught up at home before I even step out the door. By the time I'm done obsessing over it, I'm already late, I'm exhausted, and I just give up and stay home. So, yes, to answer your question directly, it is definitely getting in the way of my work and my commitments, just like I was saying.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I really, truly couldn't tell you which one is driving the bus, so to speak. They just bleed right into one another. I don't know if it's my own eyes I'm worried about or everyone else's, because it just all feels like one big, heavy cloud hanging over me all the time, no matter where I am or who happens to be looking. I'm sorry I can't give you a more definitive answer, it's just... well, it's all just so jumbled together in my head.\n\nDoctor: When you feel upset about the scar, is your main concern that it makes you look physically unattractive, or that people will judge you negatively or think poorly of you because of it?\n\nPatient: Well... gosh, it’s funny you ask it quite like that, because... well, logically, in my rational mind—the same mind that balances those ledgers down at the floral shop to the very penny—I do know that the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it looks to me. I mean, I have enough common sense to realize I might be blowing the physical appearance of it a tiny bit out of proportion when I'm standing there in my bathroom staring at it under those bright LED lights. \n\nBut... oh, it’s just so hard to shake the feeling when I'm actually out and about. When I’m sitting there at church, you know, maybe reaching out to pass the collection plate, or when I’m hosting bridge club on a Tuesday afternoon—I always put out these little cucumber sandwiches with the crusts cut off, just the way Martha likes them, and you have to reach across the card table to deal the hands—I just keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right straight to it. It’s like it has become the very first thing they notice about me now. \n\nSo I suppose it's not exactly that I think they're judging my character, or thinking I'm a bad person or a fraud because of it. It’s just... it makes me feel physically flawed in a way that overtakes everything else. They used to look at me and see a good bookkeeper, a good friend, someone who keeps a tidy home and wears nice blouses. Now, I just feel like I'm reduced to this one ugly mark, and that's all they're looking at. Even if I know, deep down, it's probably not as glaring to the rest of the world as it is to me, I just sit there at the bridge table feeling like their eyes are completely magnetized to my wrist, and it just makes me feel so terribly unattractive and exposed.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your main concern is that the scar looks unattractive to you, or that others will notice it and judge you negatively?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I suppose it really just comes back to exactly what I was trying to explain a moment ago. If I really have to pin it down for you... well, I do know, logically speaking—with that practical bookkeeping brain of mine that I've relied on all these years—that the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it looks to me when I'm standing there analyzing it. I really do have the common sense to know that. \n\nBut the real crux of the issue, the thing that just eats away at me, is that when I'm out doing my normal activities, I just can't shake the feeling that everyone is looking at it. Like when I'm at church on Sunday mornings... we always sit in the third pew on the left, right near the big stained glass window that gets all that bright morning sun, and the minute I reach out to grab the hymnal from the back of the pew, I just feel this absolute certainty that everyone’s eyes go right straight to my wrist. Or, like I mentioned, when I'm playing bridge with the ladies. I'll be shuffling the deck—we use those nice plastic-coated cards now because Mary always manages to spill her iced tea, bless her heart—and I just keep feeling like every single woman at that table has her eyes completely glued to my arm. \n\nIt’s like it has become the very first thing they notice about me now. Before, they'd notice my outfit, or my hair, or they'd ask about my daughter's new house, but now... I just feel like they see this awful scar before they even see me as a person. So, even though I know I might be exaggerating how terrible it looks in my own head, the feeling that everyone else is noticing it first and foremost is just... well, it's completely overwhelming. It just takes over everything.\n\nDoctor: When you're most upset about the scar, is it more because you feel others are judging your appearance, or because you yourself feel disgusted or upset by how it looks?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, Doctor, I feel like we might be going around in circles just a little bit, aren't we? I'm trying my best to explain it, I really am, it's just... well, let me try to put it another way, because I want to make sure I'm giving you what you need. \n\nIf I really have to choose between the two things you just mentioned... I suppose the part that truly upsets me the most, the part that just ties my stomach up in absolute knots, is the other people. Because, like I've been saying, I do have enough sense to know that the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it looks to me. I really do know that. My logical brain—the one that's spent forty years making sure the debits and credits match up perfectly at the end of the month—knows I'm probably being a bit hard on myself when I'm alone in my bathroom looking at it. \n\nBut knowing that doesn't stop the feeling I get when I'm actually out in the world. When I’m at church on Sunday, or when I'm sitting there at bridge club with the girls... I just keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right straight to it. It doesn't matter if I logically know it's just a mark, because in that moment, I feel like it’s the very first thing they notice about me now. It's like it completely erases the rest of me. Before they see my face, before they ask how my week was, they just see this flaw. So, yes, I am upset by how it looks, but it's that terrible, overwhelming feeling of being stared at—of it being the only thing people focus on when I'm just trying to go about my normal life—that really brings me to tears.\n\nDoctor: So, when you're most upset about the scar, would you say it's mainly because you feel others are judging you for it, or because you yourself can't stand how it looks no matter where you are or who's around?\n\nPatient: Oh, dear me... Doctor, I really do feel terrible, I must be doing a dreadful job of explaining myself today, because we just keep circling right back to the exact same thing, don't we? I'm so sorry, I really am trying to give you the specific answer you're looking for, but I just don't think I can separate it out quite as neatly as you want me to. \n\nIf you're asking if I can't stand it *no matter where I am* or who's around... well, that's not quite right either. Because when I'm just sitting at home in my recliner in the evenings, say, watching Jeopardy with a cup of chamomile tea, or working on the Sunday crossword puzzle—I always do the crossword in pen, my late husband used to tease me endlessly about that, but working with numbers all day just makes you confident in your penmanship, I suppose—anyway, when I'm alone like that, I don't like the scar, certainly, but I can tolerate it. I can look down at my wrist and think, 'Well, there's that ugly mark,' and then I just go right back to my puzzle. \n\nSo it's not that I'm just sitting there in a vacuum, completely unable to stand the sight of myself when I'm all alone. It really is the being around others that makes it so unbearable. But like I've been trying to say, it’s not that I think they’re judging my character or thinking I'm a wicked person. It’s just that awful, heavy feeling that their eyes are locked onto my wrist instead of my face. I just don't know how else to describe it to you, I really don't. I don't know if that's them judging me or me just being disgusted, but it's the being looked at that makes me feel so terribly exposed and upset. I just wish I could give you a simpler answer!\n\nDoctor: When you avoid activities like church or bridge club, is it mainly to prevent people from seeing the scar, or do you also worry about how they might judge you as a person if they do see it?\n\nPatient: Well, Doctor... again, I really don't think they're judging me as a person. I don't sit there and think, 'Oh, the ladies are going to think I'm a terrible, wicked woman because of this.' It's not my character I'm worried about. It really, truly is just about preventing them from seeing it in the first place. \n\nAnd it's getting to be such a problem, it really is... to give you an idea of how much this is getting in the way, it's not even just church and bridge club anymore. I've actually been late turning in some of my bookkeeping work lately. And I *never* do that. I have three small local businesses I do the ledgers for, and I pride myself on always, always having their reconciliations done by the third of the month, like clockwork. But lately... well, I've also skipped out on my choir commitments the last couple of weeks. I sing alto, and we are right in the middle of preparing for the spring cantata, which is just going to be gorgeous this year, but I just couldn't bring myself to go. Same with the community theater down on Main Street—I usually volunteer to run the box office or do the ushering on Friday nights, and I've skipped those commitments entirely, too. \n\nAnd it's all because... well, I just get so incredibly caught up at home before I even leave the house. I'll be standing in front of my closet for what feels like hours, trying on different cardigans, trying to find a blouse with cuffs that are tight enough, just doing everything I can to make absolutely sure that my wrist won’t be noticed if I reach out to hand someone a theater program or if I have to hold my sheet music up in the choir loft. I spend so much time fussing and worrying and trying to hide it that I just run out of time and energy, and I end up calling in with some silly excuse and staying home. So, you see, it really is just the fear of the scar being seen, plain and simple.\n\nDoctor: When you imagine people seeing your scar, do you worry they might think less of you as a person, or is it more that you feel the scar is so noticeable that it makes you feel embarrassed or self-conscious, regardless of what they actually think?\n\nPatient: Oh, Doctor, bless your heart, but I really do feel like a broken record today! I know you probably have to ask these things in a certain way for your medical files—it’s just like when I have to ask my bookkeeping clients for the third time to please, please separate their personal receipts from their business ones, they get a little frustrated with me, too—but yes, it is absolutely the second thing you just said. \n\nI don't sit there worrying that they think less of my character. It’s entirely about the embarrassment. Like I’ve been trying to say, I do know the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it looks to me. I know that in my logical mind. But it just feels so glaringly noticeable to me that I can't shake the self-consciousness, regardless of what's actually going on in their heads. When I’m at church on Sunday mornings, or sitting around the card table at bridge, I just keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it. \n\nIt’s this overwhelming sensation that it’s the very first thing they notice about me now. Instead of seeing me, or asking about my grandchildren, it just feels like this big, flashing neon sign on my arm. Just the thought of someone like Mrs. Higgins at church—she sits right next to me in the alto section and has eyes like a hawk, I swear she notices if you have a single thread out of place on your collar—just the thought of her eyes darting down to my wrist makes my face burn. So, yes, it’s just that awful, self-conscious feeling that it's so incredibly noticeable, not that they think I'm a bad person.\n\nDoctor: Has worrying about your scar ever caused you to avoid important responsibilities, like work, or to withdraw from relationships with friends or family members?\n\nPatient: Well, goodness me, Doctor, I feel like I’m talking to a brick wall today! Or maybe I’m just rambling so much that you’re having trouble keeping track of it all, which I suppose I can’t blame you for. My late husband used to say I could talk the ears off a brass monkey, bless his heart. But yes, like I was saying just a few minutes ago... it absolutely has caused me to avoid my responsibilities. \n\nI mentioned the bookkeeping work earlier—I've actually been late turning in the ledgers for those three local businesses I do the accounting for. And I am *never* late. It's just so completely unlike me to miss a deadline like that. And then there are my choir commitments, skipping out on the spring cantata preparations, and the community theater down on Main Street... I've missed all of those volunteer shifts lately, too. \n\nAnd it really is all just because I get so terribly caught up at home before I even step out the door. I'll be standing there in my bedroom, trying to make absolutely certain that my wrist won't be noticed. I'll try on three different long-sleeved blouses, and then I'll worry that the cuffs are going to ride up if I have to reach out to hand someone a theater program or turn a page of sheet music, and I just start fussing and worrying. By the time I'm done obsessing over it, I'm already late, I'm exhausted, and I just give up and stay home. So, yes, to answer your question directly, it is definitely getting in the way of my work and my commitments, just like I was saying.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Well, Doctor, I feel terrible saying this again, I really do, but... well, we just talked about this, didn't we? I know I can be a bit long-winded, and I'm sure you have a lot of patients to see today, but I do feel like I'm repeating myself quite a bit here.

Because when I think about standing there in the lobby, reaching out to hand a program to a stranger, or holding up my heavy music folder under those bright sanctuary lights... my wrist would be right there, completely exposed for everyone to see. I just get so overwhelmed and anxious trying to figure out how to hide it that it’s easier just to stay home. It breaks my heart to miss the shows and the singing, it really does, but I just can't bear the thought of being up there and feeling like a spectacle.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or self-conscious in social situations generally, even when the scar isn't a factor—like speaking up in a group or eating in front of others?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. No, not at all. You see, I've actually always been quite a social butterfly, really. If you were to ask anyone in my bridge group—especially Martha, she’s known me since our girls were in Girl Scouts together back in the late eighties—she’d tell you I’ve never been shy about speaking my mind or being around folks. \n\nWhether it's discussing the new town budget at the city council meetings, which I always try to attend because numbers are my bread and butter, you know, or just chatting with the ladies at the salon, I’ve always felt perfectly comfortable in a crowd. And as for eating in front of others, oh, that’s never been a problem in the slightest. We have these wonderful potluck dinners at the church every first Sunday of the month. I always bring my green bean casserole—well, I say it's mine, but it's really just my mother's old recipe with a little extra crispy onion on top, but everyone loves it—and I can sit there at those long folding tables in the fellowship hall, eating and laughing with the pastor and the choir director without a second thought. I've never felt awkward about eating or talking or just being myself. \n\nIt’s really just... well, it’s just this silly wrist. If I could just wear a long sleeve that stayed put, or if this mark wasn't there, I'd be my usual outgoing self. I don't get nervous about people looking at *me*, exactly, or judging what I have to say... it's just about them looking at *it*. Does that make sense? Because before this whole thing started bothering me so much, I was always the very first one to raise my hand to organize the holiday bake sale or stand up in front of everyone to give the treasurer's report...\n\nDoctor: When you worry about people noticing your scar, do you feel like they might judge you negatively as a person—like they might think you’re less capable, unattractive, or strange—or do you mostly feel like they’re just noticing the scar without it affecting how they see you overall?\n\nPatient: Well, goodness, doctor, I’m really not sure how to answer that. I don't know if I've ever really stopped to parse out exactly what's going through their heads, to be perfectly honest with you. \n\nI mean, I certainly hope they don't think I'm incapable! The people I work for, like the hardware store owner I mentioned, and I also do the ledgers for the little floral shop down on Main Street—I’ve been doing their books since 2012. Or maybe it was late 2011? No, it was definitely 2012, because that was the year my daughter got married and we used them for the centerpieces, those lovely hydrangeas. Anyway, my point is, the people in this town know my work is impeccable. They know I'm sharp as a tack. So I don't think they look at my wrist and suddenly think I've forgotten how to balance a spreadsheet or run a payroll. \n\nBut as for whether they think I'm unattractive or strange... I just don't know. That’s the thing that really gets my mind spinning, you see. I just don't know *what* they're thinking. I'll be at the teller window at the bank, handing over a stack of deposit slips for one of my clients, and I’ll see the teller's eyes flick down to my hand. And I just freeze. Are they disgusted? Are they wondering what on earth happened to me? Do they think I'm unkempt or careless to have a mark like that right out in the open? \n\nI couldn't tell you for sure if they're judging my whole character, or if they're just surprised by the scar and then moving on. I really, truly don't know. I just know I can't stand the thought of their eyes lingering on it. It makes me feel so exposed. It’s like... well, it’s like all the good things about me, all my years of hard work and my nice outfits and the fact that I'm a good neighbor, it all just fades into the background, and all that's left for them to see is this... this flaw. So I suppose I don't really have a good answer for you about whether they're judging me as a person. I just know that the looking itself is what I can't abide.\n\nDoctor: When you think about how much the scar bothers you, does it feel like it's about the way the scar looks, or is it more about what you imagine people might think or do when they see it?\n\nPatient: Oh, my... well, that’s a very nuanced question, isn't it? I’m really not sure how to separate the two, if I'm being completely honest. I don't think I've ever really thought to untangle it like that. \n\nI mean, on the one hand, I look at it myself and I just... I despise how it looks. I’ll be standing in my master bathroom—we actually just remodeled it a few years ago, put in those very bright, daylight LED bulbs around the vanity because the old fixtures were from the nineties and were so terribly dim I couldn't see a thing when I was putting on my moisturizer in the mornings—and I’ll catch sight of my wrist in that harsh, unforgiving light. And it just looks so glaring and awful to me. Just the physical appearance of it bothers me to my very core. I'll just stand there by the sink, sometimes for ten or fifteen minutes, rubbing my thumb over it and thinking about how it just ruins the whole line of my arm. \n\nBut then, on the exact same day, I’ll be down at the grocery store... say I'm at the deli counter waiting on a half pound of sliced turkey, the mesquite smoked kind because that's the only one that makes a decent sandwich for lunch, in my opinion... and I'll reach up to take the little plastic bag over the counter, and I'll realize my sleeve has ridden up. And suddenly my heart is just in my throat, pounding away, because I'm absolutely paralyzed by the thought of the deli clerk or the woman with the shopping cart behind me catching a glimpse of it. \n\nSo is it the look of it that upsets me, or the fear of what they'll think when they see it? I really, truly couldn't tell you which one is driving the bus, so to speak. They just bleed right into one another. I don't know if it's my own eyes I'm worried about or everyone else's, because it just all feels like one big, heavy cloud hanging over me all the time, no matter where I am or who happens to be looking. I'm sorry I can't give you a more definitive answer, it's just... well, it's all just so jumbled together in my head.\n\nDoctor: When you feel upset about the scar, is your main concern that it makes you look physically unattractive, or that people will judge you negatively or think poorly of you because of it?\n\nPatient: Well... gosh, it’s funny you ask it quite like that, because... well, logically, in my rational mind—the same mind that balances those ledgers down at the floral shop to the very penny—I do know that the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it looks to me. I mean, I have enough common sense to realize I might be blowing the physical appearance of it a tiny bit out of proportion when I'm standing there in my bathroom staring at it under those bright LED lights. \n\nBut... oh, it’s just so hard to shake the feeling when I'm actually out and about. When I’m sitting there at church, you know, maybe reaching out to pass the collection plate, or when I’m hosting bridge club on a Tuesday afternoon—I always put out these little cucumber sandwiches with the crusts cut off, just the way Martha likes them, and you have to reach across the card table to deal the hands—I just keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right straight to it. It’s like it has become the very first thing they notice about me now. \n\nSo I suppose it's not exactly that I think they're judging my character, or thinking I'm a bad person or a fraud because of it. It’s just... it makes me feel physically flawed in a way that overtakes everything else. They used to look at me and see a good bookkeeper, a good friend, someone who keeps a tidy home and wears nice blouses. Now, I just feel like I'm reduced to this one ugly mark, and that's all they're looking at. Even if I know, deep down, it's probably not as glaring to the rest of the world as it is to me, I just sit there at the bridge table feeling like their eyes are completely magnetized to my wrist, and it just makes me feel so terribly unattractive and exposed.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your main concern is that the scar looks unattractive to you, or that others will notice it and judge you negatively?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I suppose it really just comes back to exactly what I was trying to explain a moment ago. If I really have to pin it down for you... well, I do know, logically speaking—with that practical bookkeeping brain of mine that I've relied on all these years—that the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it looks to me when I'm standing there analyzing it. I really do have the common sense to know that. \n\nBut the real crux of the issue, the thing that just eats away at me, is that when I'm out doing my normal activities, I just can't shake the feeling that everyone is looking at it. Like when I'm at church on Sunday mornings... we always sit in the third pew on the left, right near the big stained glass window that gets all that bright morning sun, and the minute I reach out to grab the hymnal from the back of the pew, I just feel this absolute certainty that everyone’s eyes go right straight to my wrist. Or, like I mentioned, when I'm playing bridge with the ladies. I'll be shuffling the deck—we use those nice plastic-coated cards now because Mary always manages to spill her iced tea, bless her heart—and I just keep feeling like every single woman at that table has her eyes completely glued to my arm. \n\nIt’s like it has become the very first thing they notice about me now. Before, they'd notice my outfit, or my hair, or they'd ask about my daughter's new house, but now... I just feel like they see this awful scar before they even see me as a person. So, even though I know I might be exaggerating how terrible it looks in my own head, the feeling that everyone else is noticing it first and foremost is just... well, it's completely overwhelming. It just takes over everything.\n\nDoctor: When you're most upset about the scar, is it more because you feel others are judging your appearance, or because you yourself feel disgusted or upset by how it looks?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, Doctor, I feel like we might be going around in circles just a little bit, aren't we? I'm trying my best to explain it, I really am, it's just... well, let me try to put it another way, because I want to make sure I'm giving you what you need. \n\nIf I really have to choose between the two things you just mentioned... I suppose the part that truly upsets me the most, the part that just ties my stomach up in absolute knots, is the other people. Because, like I've been saying, I do have enough sense to know that the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it looks to me. I really do know that. My logical brain—the one that's spent forty years making sure the debits and credits match up perfectly at the end of the month—knows I'm probably being a bit hard on myself when I'm alone in my bathroom looking at it. \n\nBut knowing that doesn't stop the feeling I get when I'm actually out in the world. When I’m at church on Sunday, or when I'm sitting there at bridge club with the girls... I just keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right straight to it. It doesn't matter if I logically know it's just a mark, because in that moment, I feel like it’s the very first thing they notice about me now. It's like it completely erases the rest of me. Before they see my face, before they ask how my week was, they just see this flaw. So, yes, I am upset by how it looks, but it's that terrible, overwhelming feeling of being stared at—of it being the only thing people focus on when I'm just trying to go about my normal life—that really brings me to tears.\n\nDoctor: So, when you're most upset about the scar, would you say it's mainly because you feel others are judging you for it, or because you yourself can't stand how it looks no matter where you are or who's around?\n\nPatient: Oh, dear me... Doctor, I really do feel terrible, I must be doing a dreadful job of explaining myself today, because we just keep circling right back to the exact same thing, don't we? I'm so sorry, I really am trying to give you the specific answer you're looking for, but I just don't think I can separate it out quite as neatly as you want me to. \n\nIf you're asking if I can't stand it *no matter where I am* or who's around... well, that's not quite right either. Because when I'm just sitting at home in my recliner in the evenings, say, watching Jeopardy with a cup of chamomile tea, or working on the Sunday crossword puzzle—I always do the crossword in pen, my late husband used to tease me endlessly about that, but working with numbers all day just makes you confident in your penmanship, I suppose—anyway, when I'm alone like that, I don't like the scar, certainly, but I can tolerate it. I can look down at my wrist and think, 'Well, there's that ugly mark,' and then I just go right back to my puzzle. \n\nSo it's not that I'm just sitting there in a vacuum, completely unable to stand the sight of myself when I'm all alone. It really is the being around others that makes it so unbearable. But like I've been trying to say, it’s not that I think they’re judging my character or thinking I'm a wicked person. It’s just that awful, heavy feeling that their eyes are locked onto my wrist instead of my face. I just don't know how else to describe it to you, I really don't. I don't know if that's them judging me or me just being disgusted, but it's the being looked at that makes me feel so terribly exposed and upset. I just wish I could give you a simpler answer!\n\nDoctor: When you avoid activities like church or bridge club, is it mainly to prevent people from seeing the scar, or do you also worry about how they might judge you as a person if they do see it?\n\nPatient: Well, Doctor... again, I really don't think they're judging me as a person. I don't sit there and think, 'Oh, the ladies are going to think I'm a terrible, wicked woman because of this.' It's not my character I'm worried about. It really, truly is just about preventing them from seeing it in the first place. \n\nAnd it's getting to be such a problem, it really is... to give you an idea of how much this is getting in the way, it's not even just church and bridge club anymore. I've actually been late turning in some of my bookkeeping work lately. And I *never* do that. I have three small local businesses I do the ledgers for, and I pride myself on always, always having their reconciliations done by the third of the month, like clockwork. But lately... well, I've also skipped out on my choir commitments the last couple of weeks. I sing alto, and we are right in the middle of preparing for the spring cantata, which is just going to be gorgeous this year, but I just couldn't bring myself to go. Same with the community theater down on Main Street—I usually volunteer to run the box office or do the ushering on Friday nights, and I've skipped those commitments entirely, too. \n\nAnd it's all because... well, I just get so incredibly caught up at home before I even leave the house. I'll be standing in front of my closet for what feels like hours, trying on different cardigans, trying to find a blouse with cuffs that are tight enough, just doing everything I can to make absolutely sure that my wrist won’t be noticed if I reach out to hand someone a theater program or if I have to hold my sheet music up in the choir loft. I spend so much time fussing and worrying and trying to hide it that I just run out of time and energy, and I end up calling in with some silly excuse and staying home. So, you see, it really is just the fear of the scar being seen, plain and simple.\n\nDoctor: When you imagine people seeing your scar, do you worry they might think less of you as a person, or is it more that you feel the scar is so noticeable that it makes you feel embarrassed or self-conscious, regardless of what they actually think?\n\nPatient: Oh, Doctor, bless your heart, but I really do feel like a broken record today! I know you probably have to ask these things in a certain way for your medical files—it’s just like when I have to ask my bookkeeping clients for the third time to please, please separate their personal receipts from their business ones, they get a little frustrated with me, too—but yes, it is absolutely the second thing you just said. \n\nI don't sit there worrying that they think less of my character. It’s entirely about the embarrassment. Like I’ve been trying to say, I do know the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it looks to me. I know that in my logical mind. But it just feels so glaringly noticeable to me that I can't shake the self-consciousness, regardless of what's actually going on in their heads. When I’m at church on Sunday mornings, or sitting around the card table at bridge, I just keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it. \n\nIt’s this overwhelming sensation that it’s the very first thing they notice about me now. Instead of seeing me, or asking about my grandchildren, it just feels like this big, flashing neon sign on my arm. Just the thought of someone like Mrs. Higgins at church—she sits right next to me in the alto section and has eyes like a hawk, I swear she notices if you have a single thread out of place on your collar—just the thought of her eyes darting down to my wrist makes my face burn. So, yes, it’s just that awful, self-conscious feeling that it's so incredibly noticeable, not that they think I'm a bad person.\n\nDoctor: Has worrying about your scar ever caused you to avoid important responsibilities, like work, or to withdraw from relationships with friends or family members?\n\nPatient: Well, goodness me, Doctor, I feel like I’m talking to a brick wall today! Or maybe I’m just rambling so much that you’re having trouble keeping track of it all, which I suppose I can’t blame you for. My late husband used to say I could talk the ears off a brass monkey, bless his heart. But yes, like I was saying just a few minutes ago... it absolutely has caused me to avoid my responsibilities. \n\nI mentioned the bookkeeping work earlier—I've actually been late turning in the ledgers for those three local businesses I do the accounting for. And I am *never* late. It's just so completely unlike me to miss a deadline like that. And then there are my choir commitments, skipping out on the spring cantata preparations, and the community theater down on Main Street... I've missed all of those volunteer shifts lately, too. \n\nAnd it really is all just because I get so terribly caught up at home before I even step out the door. I'll be standing there in my bedroom, trying to make absolutely certain that my wrist won't be noticed. I'll try on three different long-sleeved blouses, and then I'll worry that the cuffs are going to ride up if I have to reach out to hand someone a theater program or turn a page of sheet music, and I just start fussing and worrying. By the time I'm done obsessing over it, I'm already late, I'm exhausted, and I just give up and stay home. So, yes, to answer your question directly, it is definitely getting in the way of my work and my commitments, just like I was saying.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that the concern about your scar was so intense or persistent that it felt like it was controlling your life, making it hard to focus on anything else when you were around other people?\n\nPatient: Well, Doctor, I feel terrible saying this again, I really do, but... well, we just talked about this, didn't we? I know I can be a bit long-winded, and I'm sure you have a lot of patients to see today, but I do feel like I'm repeating myself quite a bit here. \n\nBut to answer your question, yes, it absolutely feels that way. Like I was just saying a few minutes ago, I do know, in my logical mind, that the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it looks to me. I know that. But when I'm out and about... well, like I said about being at church on Sunday mornings, or sitting around the card table at bridge club, I just keep feeling like everyone's eyes go right to it. \n\nIt's just this overwhelming feeling that it's the very first thing they notice about me now. It makes it nearly impossible to focus on the sermon, or remember what cards have been played, because I'm just so consumed by the thought that they're all staring at my wrist. So, yes, it definitely feels like it's controlling things and making it hard to focus, just like I was trying to explain before."
